=== PROMPT ===
You will be given a competitive programming problem.
You are given an array of integers nums. Some values in nums are missing and are denoted by -1.
You can choose a pair of positive integers (x, y) exactly once and replace each missing element with either x or y.
You need to minimize the maximum absolute difference between adjacent elements of nums after replacements.
Return the minimum possible difference.
 
Example 1:

Input: nums = [1,2,-1,10,8]
Output: 4
Explanation:
By choosing the pair as (6, 7), nums can be changed to [1, 2, 6, 10, 8].
The absolute differences between adjacent elements are:

|1 - 2| == 1
|2 - 6| == 4
|6 - 10| == 4
|10 - 8| == 2


Example 2:

Input: nums = [-1,-1,-1]
Output: 0
Explanation:
By choosing the pair as (4, 4), nums can be changed to [4, 4, 4].

Example 3:

Input: nums = [-1,10,-1,8]
Output: 1
Explanation:
By choosing the pair as (11, 9), nums can be changed to [11, 10, 9, 8].

 
Constraints:

2 <= nums.length <= 10^5
nums[i] is either -1 or in the range [1, 10^9].

Use the following starter code to write the final solution.
```python
class Solution:
    def minDifference(self, nums: List[int]) -> int:
        
```

Reason in a commit-implement-recheck way.

A productive rhythm for this task usually looks like this:
- Isolate the decisive constraints, the target complexity, and at most one or two approaches that are still truly plausible.
- If two candidates remain, compare them only on the decisive constraint and commit quickly to the stronger one instead of keeping a wide search open.
- Before coding, keep only a tiny implementation ledger for the chosen plan: main invariant, key state or transition, and the one or two edge cases most likely to fail.
- Move into code as soon as that ledger is clear. After coding starts, keep reasoning attached to the current draft rather than reopening the high-level search.
- After the first complete draft, do one short implementation-check-adjust-check loop on the same draft: test the key edge case, patch locally if needed, and recheck.
- Reopen the main design only if a concrete counterexample breaks the core invariant. Otherwise prefer the local patch and keep closing.
- Once the current draft, invariant, and short recheck still agree, finalize the solution.

Output only the final Python code enclosed in a code block.

Let's step back.

We have a set of gap constraints, each is a subset of pairs (x,y). Need intersection. Need efficient check.

Maybe there is a simpler way: Because x,y only two values, we can choose them; each gap constraint can be expressed as "x or y must lie in some interval" plus "if x,y both used in a multi gap, distance <=D". The same x/y can satisfy multiple gaps independently.

Let's characterize all constraints as boolean combinations of membership of x and y in intervals:
- Prefix/suffix: x∈I or y∈I.
- Single gap: x∈J or y∈J.
- Multi gap: (x∈J or y∈J) OR ( (x∈A or y∈A) & (x∈B or y∈B) & |x-y|<=D ).

All are symmetric. Need decide if ∃x,y.

This is a 2D existence problem with intervals and one distance condition. Could solve by considering cases based on whether x and y are "close" (|x-y|<=D) or not.

Case 1: |x-y| <= D. Then for multi gaps, condition simplifies? If |x-y|<=D, multi gap condition becomes (x∈A or y∈A) & (x∈B or y∈B), because if x∈J then also covered; but if x∈J, then x∈A and x∈B, so the latter holds. Actually if |x-y|<=D, C_multi = (x∈A or y∈A) & (x∈B or y∈B). Since J subset A∩B; if x∈J then both hold. If y∈J both hold. So yes. Single gap remains x∈J or y∈J. Prefix x∈I or y∈I. Thus in close case, all constraints are of form "interval H contains x or y" (unary interval coverage). Because for multi gap, need two such constraints: H=A and H=B. Single gap H=J. Prefix H=I. Known-known already checked. So close case: Need ∃x,y with |x-y|<=D and for every required interval H_i, x∈H_i or y∈H_i. (For multi gap, two intervals A and B both required; for single gap, one interval J; prefix/suffix one interval I.) Let's call these "required intervals" R_i.

Case 2: |x-y| > D. Then multi gap condition cannot use different endpoints; it requires x∈J or y∈J (all unknowns same). Single/prefix same unary. So far case: Need ∃x,y with |x-y|>D and for every required interval H_i, x∈H_i or y∈H_i, where for multi gaps H_i=J (intersection), not A and B. Wait if |x-y|>D, multi gap condition is x∈J or y∈J. So required interval is J. For single gap also J. Prefix/suffix I. Known-known no. Thus far case: Need ∃x,y with |x-y|>D and every required interval H_i contains x or y.

But note if |x-y|>D, any multi gap with endpoints covered by different values impossible, so both endpoints must be covered by same value, i.e., that value in J. Yes.

So both cases reduce to:
Given a set of intervals H_i (positive integer intervals), can we choose two numbers x,y (possibly with constraint |x-y| <= D or >D) such that every H_i contains at least one of x,y?

Classic greedy for two points:

But note for Mode F, interval set for multi gaps is J, while for Mode C is I_L and I_R. If a single x covers all close-mode intervals, it also covers all far-mode intervals? For multi gap, J subset I_L∩I_R, so if x in I_L and I_R then x in J. Yes. Thus common intersection close implies common intersection far. So we can just build close-mode intervals for common intersection? But for two-point far, we need far-mode intervals (J for multi gaps) because if |x-y|>D, multi gaps require one value in J, not just endpoints separately. So we need maintain two interval sets or decide modes.

Maybe there is an even more direct condition using interval stabbing.

Let's define:
- Common intervals C (for close mode): includes for each gap:
   * prefix/suffix: I_endpoint
   * single: J
   * multi: I_L and I_R
- Far intervals F (for far mode): includes:
   * prefix/suffix: I_endpoint
   * single: J
   * multi: J (if nonempty)
Need check either:
(A) ∃ x,y with |x-y|<=D that stab all intervals in C.
(B) ∃ x,y with |x-y|>D that stab all intervals in F.
Because if |x-y|<=D, multi gaps can use different values; if >D, multi gaps must use same value per gap, so J.

But wait: What if |x-y|<=D but for a multi gap we choose to use same value x in J; then close-mode intervals I_L and I_R are both covered by x. So C intervals okay. What if |x-y|>D but for a multi gap J empty, F impossible. Good.

Is there a possibility with |x-y|>D and multi gap where endpoints covered by different values but path still valid because k>=2 and unknown-unknown edges maybe not require |x-y|<=D if we don't place x and y adjacent? But if both values are used in the same gap, since the sequence of unknowns is contiguous, at some point there is an adjacent pair with different values (unless all unknowns same). If |x-y|>D, that edge violates. Could we use only one value in each gap but different gaps use different values. Then for that gap, the value used must be in J. So F interval J. Good.

Now, how to check two-point stabbing with distance constraint efficiently for a list of intervals.

Maybe we can avoid binary search by computing minimal D? But binary search with O(n log V) and O(n) check is fine. Need implement check(D) robust.

Let's first solve two-point stabbing with distance constraint.

Given intervals list H (maybe up to O(n) intervals). Need determine if ∃ x,y positive satisfying:
1. For all intervals, x∈I or y∈I.
2. |x-y| <= D (close) OR >D (far).

We can assume x<=y by swapping. Let's derive an O(m log m) or O(m) check per D. m up to n + gaps*2 <= 2e5. Binary search 31, O(m log m) okay ~6e6 log maybe fine in Python? 2e5*31*log ~ 1e8 maybe borderline but okay if optimized? Need aim O(m) per check.

We can precompute intervals? D changes, intervals depend on D. Need recompute per check. O(n log n) per check maybe 31*1e5 log = 50 million, okay in Python if optimized? Need be careful.

Let's derive O(m) two-point check.

For a set of intervals [l_i, r_i] (integers, l<=r), define:
Rmin = min r_i
Lmax = max l_i
If Rmin >= Lmax, then common intersection nonempty (any point between), so feasible for both close/far? For close yes; for far if common intersection nonempty, choose x in intersection and y far? Wait for far mode, if x in all intervals, y far works. So feasible. Thus if common intersection nonempty, return true for either mode. But for far mode, if x in all intervals, y far; yes. So we can early return true.

If Rmin < Lmax, no single point covers all. Need two points. For x<=y, conditions as above:
- x <= Rmin (otherwise intervals ending before x impossible)
- y >= Lmax (otherwise intervals starting after y impossible)
- For all intervals with l_i > x, need y <= r_i (so y covers them). Equiv y <= min_{i: l_i > x} r_i. Let Rright(x) = min r_i among intervals with l_i > x. If no such intervals, Rright=∞.
- Also need y >= x (since ordered). But if y >= Lmax and Lmax might be <x? If x<=Rmin and Rmin<Lmax, then x<=Rmin<Lmax<=y, so y>=x automatically. If common intersection nonempty we early return; for no common intersection, Rmin < Lmax, so any feasible y>=Lmax > Rmin >= x, so y>=x. Good. Thus in the hard case Rmin < Lmax, we can ignore y>=x.
- Need x <= Rmin and x positive. Since Rmin maybe from intervals; positive.
- Need y in [Lmax, Rright(x)] nonempty, i.e., Rright(x) >= Lmax.
Thus existence of x<=Rmin such that Rright(x) >= Lmax. Then y can be chosen in [Lmax, Rright(x)]. Distance constraints:
   * Close: need y - x <= D. Since y can be any in [Lmax, Rright(x)], and x fixed. Need min possible y = Lmax (because Lmax > x). So close feasible iff ∃ x<=Rmin with Rright(x) >= Lmax and Lmax - x <= D. (Since choose y=Lmax; y>=Lmax; if Lmax<=Rright). Note if Lmax - x <=D.
   * Far: need y - x > D. Need ∃ x<=Rmin with Rright(x) >= Lmax and there exists y∈[Lmax, Rright(x)] with y - x > D. Since max y = Rright(x). Feasible iff Rright(x) - x > D. (Choose y=Rright(x) if >x+D; if Rright=∞, yes). Also need y>=Lmax; if Rright>=Lmax. So far feasible iff ∃ x<=Rmin with Rright(x) >= Lmax and Rright(x) - x > D.

But wait if Rright(x)=∞ (x covers all intervals? Actually if no interval with l_i > x, then x lies in all intervals? Since x<=Rmin and l_i<=x for all i, yes x in all intervals. Then common intersection nonempty? If no l_i>x and x<=Rmin, then x is common. But we are in Rmin<Lmax, so there is some l_i=Lmax > Rmin >= x, so there is interval with l_i > x. Thus Rright finite. So no ∞ in hard case. Good.

So the problem reduces to computing maximum over x<=Rmin of Rright(x) - x (for far) and checking Lmax - x <=D (for close) with Rright(x)>=Lmax.

But is this characterization complete? Let's test with intervals [1,2], [50,60], [100,200]. Rmin=2, Lmax=100. For x<=2, intervals with l_i>x: [50,60] and [100,200], Rright=min(60,200)=60 < Lmax, no feasible. Good.

Now, let's deeply verify the reduction from gap constraints to interval stabbing with modes.

Detailed derivation:

Given D. For each missing block:
- If block at beginning before known R: Need assign values to k unknowns. Since no left constraint, only last unknown adjacent to R. If |x-y|<=D, we can set last unknown to a value in I_R; if neither x nor y in I_R impossible. If |x-y|>D, all unknowns in block must be same (otherwise adjacent diff >D); set all to value in I_R. Thus condition: x∈I_R or y∈I_R. So interval I_R.
- Suffix analogous.
- Block between known L,R:
   k=1: One unknown adjacent to both. Need value v∈{x,y} with |L-v|<=D and |v-R|<=D, i.e., v∈I_L∩I_R=J. Condition x∈J or y∈J. No distance condition.
   k>=2:
      If |x-y|<=D: We can choose first unknown value a∈{x,y}∩I_L and last unknown value b∈{x,y}∩I_R. Since k>=2, there is at least one edge between unknowns; if a=b, all a; if a≠b, edge diff |x-y|<=D. Thus condition (x∈I_L or y∈I_L) and (x∈I_R or y∈I_R). This is exactly two intervals I_L,I_R covered by x/y.
      If |x-y|>D: All unknowns in block must be equal, so need a value v∈{x,y}∩I_L∩I_R=J. Condition x∈J or y∈J.
Thus for a fixed pair, if |x-y|<=D, all multi gaps require their endpoint intervals covered. If |x-y|>D, all multi gaps require their intersection interval covered. Prefix/suffix/single always require their interval covered. This is exactly Mode C/F.

Now, two_point_stabbing with distance constraint:
Given intervals H. Need ∃ x,y positive with every interval containing x or y, and |x-y| <= D (close) or >D (far). We can assume x<=y.

Let's prove the conditions for hard case Rmin < Lmax.

Let Rmin = min r_i, Lmax = max l_i.
If Rmin >= Lmax, choose x = Lmax (or any in [Lmax,Rmin]) in all intervals. For close, y=x; for far, choose y = x + D + 1 (positive) (or if x+D+1 maybe; positive). Then x covers all intervals, y arbitrary. Feasible. Note if D maybe large, y positive. So return True.

If Rmin < Lmax:
For any feasible x<=y:
- x must be <= Rmin. Proof: Suppose x > Rmin. Let interval j with r_j=Rmin. Since r_j < x, x not in interval. Since y>=x>r_j, y not in interval. Contradiction. Thus x<=Rmin.
- y must be >= Lmax. Proof: Suppose y < Lmax. Let interval j with l_j=Lmax. Since l_j > y, y not in interval. Since x<=y<l_j, x not in interval. Contradiction. Thus y>=Lmax.
- For any interval with l_i > x, x not in it. It must be covered by y, so y∈[l_i,r_i], hence y<=r_i. Therefore y <= Rright(x) = min_{i: l_i > x} r_i. Conversely, if x<=Rmin, y>=Lmax, and y<=Rright(x), then:
   * For intervals with l_i <= x: since x<=Rmin<=r_i, x∈interval.
   * For intervals with l_i > x: since y>=Lmax>=l_i (because Lmax is max l) and y<=Rright(x)<=r_i, y∈interval.
Thus feasible iff x<=Rmin, y>=Lmax, y<=Rright(x). Also y>=x automatically because Lmax > Rmin >= x? Wait Rmin < Lmax, x<=Rmin, so y>=Lmax > x. Good.

Now, Rright(x) = min r_i among l_i > x. It is nondecreasing as x increases (set shrinks). The condition Rright(x) >= Lmax is necessary for existence of y. Let T = max{ l_i | r_i < Lmax }. Then for any x:
Rright(x) >= Lmax iff no interval with l_i > x has r_i < Lmax, iff x >= l_i for all intervals with r_i < Lmax, iff x >= T. (If no bad intervals, T=-∞/0; but if Rmin<Lmax, there is at least one interval with r_i < Lmax? Since Rmin<Lmax, the interval with min r has r=Rmin<Lmax, so yes T exists.)
Thus feasible x must be in [T, Rmin]. Note T<=Rmin necessary for any feasibility.

Close mode: Need y - x <= D. Since y can be chosen as Lmax (which is >=Lmax and <=Rright if Rright>=Lmax), and Lmax > x, the minimal distance for a given x is Lmax - x. Thus close feasible iff ∃ x∈[T,Rmin] with Lmax - x <= D, i.e., x >= Lmax - D. Since x positive, lower = max(1, T, Lmax-D) <= Rmin. This is O(1) after computing T,Rmin,Lmax. But wait is Lmax always <= Rright(x) for x∈[T,Rmin]? Yes by T. So y=Lmax works. Need y positive; Lmax is max l_i, intervals clipped to positive, so Lmax>=1. Good.
But is it possible to choose y < Lmax and x > y? We assumed x<=y. Since pair unordered, we can always label smaller as x. In hard case any feasible pair has smaller <=Rmin < Lmax <= larger, so the smaller is x and larger y. Thus y>=Lmax. Good.

Far mode: Need y - x > D. For a given x∈[T,Rmin], y can be any in [Lmax, Rright(x)]. To maximize distance, choose y=Rright(x). Far feasible iff ∃ x∈[T,Rmin] with Rright(x) - x > D. Need compute max_{x∈[T,Rmin]} Rright(x) - x.

Now, compute max f efficiently. We can sort intervals by l. But per feasibility check we already build intervals; sorting O(m log m) maybe okay? But we can perhaps avoid sorting by using T and Rmin? Need compute max f. Let's see if max f has a simple formula.

Rright(x) = min r_i among intervals with l_i > x. For x in [T,Rmin], all bad intervals (r<Lmax) have l<=x, so excluded. The intervals with l_i > x all have r_i >= Lmax. Rright(x) is the minimum r among intervals starting after x. We want max of (min r after x) - x.

This is like for each interval j considered as the one with minimum r among those to the right, f = r_j - x, where x must be less than l_j? Actually for Rright(x) to equal r_j, j is the interval with smallest r among l_i > x. So x must be < l_j (strictly, because l_i > x) and x >= all l_k for intervals k with r_k < r_j? More complex.

Maybe we can compute max f by scanning intervals sorted by l and suffix min r. O(m log m) per check. Since m up to ~2e5 and binary search 31, sorting each check would be O(31*n log n) ~ 31*2e5*18 ~ 111 million comparisons, maybe okay in Py? Might be borderline but acceptable if optimized? But we can do better by not sorting per check? Intervals depend on D but their left/right endpoints are known±D. Sorting by l changes with D? l_i = known-D or max(1, known-D) or J lower. For intervals from known values, all left endpoints are known-D (clipped), so order by l is same as order by known value (since subtract same D). For J intervals, lower = max(L-D,R-D) = max(L,R)-D? Actually J = [max(L-D,R-D), min(L+D,R+D)] = [max(L,R)-D, min(L,R)+D] if intervals overlap. Its left endpoint order depends on max(L,R)-D, which is same as order by max(L,R) (or known value). Clipping at 1 could alter if known-D<1. But known values positive; if D large, many lower clipped to1, order changes. However we can maybe avoid sorting by using global min/max? Let's explore.

Maybe there is a simpler formula for max f:
We need choose x<=Rmin. Rright(x) is min r among intervals with l>x. The maximum of Rright(x)-x. Since Rright(x) is a step function nondecreasing, and -x decreasing, the maximum occurs just after some left endpoint? Actually at x = l_p (smallest in step) as argued. So we can evaluate at left endpoints <=Rmin. Number of unique left endpoints O(m). Need sort by l. But maybe we can compute max f using only Lmax and Rmin? Let's derive.

For any x, Rright(x) >= Lmax. To maximize Rright(x)-x, we want large Rright and small x. Rright is at most? It is min r among intervals to the right. The largest possible Rright for a given x is maybe the minimum r among intervals with l>x. If there is an interval with very large r but also a small r to its right, Rright small. The maximum over x of Rright(x)-x might be achieved by x = T? Let's test. Since Rright nondecreasing and -x decreasing, f may not be monotonic. Example intervals [1,100], [50,60], [80,120]. Rmin=60? min r=60, Lmax=80. T = max l with r<80: [1,100] r100 no, [50,60] r60 l50, [80,120] r120 no => T=50. Rmin=60. Evaluate x=50: intervals l>50: [80,120], Rright=120, f=70. x=60? x<=Rmin=60; intervals l>60: [80,120], Rright=120, f=60. So max at T=50. T is a left endpoint of bad interval. Could max occur at x > T not a left endpoint? f decreases within step, so no. Could occur at x = T if T not a left endpoint? T is max l of bad intervals, so a left endpoint. Good.

Maybe max f = max_{bad interval?} something? Let's examine. Rright(x) is min r among intervals with l>x. For x in [T,Rmin], the set of intervals with l>x excludes all bad intervals (r<Lmax). Thus all intervals in the set have r>=Lmax. Rright is the minimum r among intervals with l>x. The interval attaining Rright could be a "good" interval with smallest r among those starting after x. To maximize f, we might want x just below the left endpoint of that interval, i.e., x = max l among intervals with r < r_j? Hmm.

Let's derive an alternative using sorted by r maybe.

But maybe we don't need far mode check separately? Could Mode F be subsumed by Mode C with a different D? No, far mode allows x,y far apart, which can satisfy disjoint intervals with one value each; close mode requires them close. Need check.

Can we compute far feasibility simply as: There exists x∈[T,Rmin] and Rright(x) > x + D. Since Rright(x) is min r of intervals to the right. This is equivalent to there exists an interval j such that r_j > x + D and all intervals with l > x have r >= r_j? Actually Rright(x) = r_j for some j. If Rright(x) - x > D, then the interval j with minimum r among l>x has r_j > x+D. That means every interval with l > x has r > x+D. In particular, the interval with smallest r to the right starts after x and ends after x+D. This is like there is a gap of length >D between x and all right intervals? Let's think.

For far mode, we need choose x and y with y-x>D. y must be <= r_i for all intervals with l_i>x. Thus y-x>D means for all intervals with l_i>x, r_i - x > D. Equiv x < r_i - D for all intervals with l_i>x. Since x is less than their l_i too. So x must be less than min(l_i, r_i-D) for all intervals to the right. Actually if x is to the left of an interval, to have y > x+D and y<=r_i, need r_i - x > D, i.e., x < r_i - D. Since l_i > x automatically. So for every interval with l_i>x, x < r_i - D. Also x>=T.

Notice all intervals are centered at known values with radius D, or intersections of two such intervals. The left endpoint is essentially max(1, value - D) for I, or max(1, max(L,R)-D) for J. The right endpoint is value + D or min(L,R)+D. Thus left endpoints are monotonic with some underlying "anchor" value (the known value or max of pair). Sorting by left endpoint is same as sorting by anchor value, except clipping at 1: if anchor-D <=1, left=1 for many anchors. But if D large enough that many lefts clip to1, their order by anchor doesn't matter for left; but right endpoints differ. Thus intervals with l=1 are always in the left-covered set for any x>=1? If x=1, yes; if x>1, l=1<=x. So they never contribute to Rright. Let's implement two_point_stabbing(intervals, D, require_far):
Input list of [l,r] clipped positive.
If not intervals: return True (for both close/far? For far, if no intervals, choose x=1,y=D+2; yes if D maybe 10^9, y=1e9+2 positive. If D=10^9, y=1000000002; allowed positive no upper bound. Good.)
Compute Rmin = min r, Lmax = max l.
If Rmin >= Lmax:
   # common intersection nonempty
   return True (for both modes). For far, choose x = Lmax (or Rmin) in all intervals, y = x + D + 1. But wait if x+D+1 maybe not positive? x>=1, D>=0, yes. If D=10^9, y up to 2e9+1, allowed. Good.
If Rmin < Lmax:
   # Need two points, x<=y, x<=Rmin, y>=Lmax.
   # Compute T = max l_i for intervals with r_i < Lmax.
   T = -inf
   for l,r in intervals:
       if r < Lmax and l > T: T = l
   # If T > Rmin: no two-point stabbing at all (regardless distance) because no x can satisfy Rright>=Lmax.
   if T > Rmin: return False
   if not require_far:
       # close: need x >= Lmax - D and x >= T and x<=Rmin
       lower = max(1, T, Lmax - D)
       return lower <= Rmin
   else:
       # far: need max_{x in [T,Rmin]} Rright(x)-x > D
       # Sort intervals by l ascending; compute suffix min r.
       # But we only need intervals with l maybe >? Let's implement.
       intervals_sorted = sorted(intervals, key=lambda iv: iv[0])  # l
       m = len(intervals_sorted)
       suffix_min = [0]*(m+1)
       suffix_min[m] = INF
       for i in range(m-1, -1, -1):
           r = intervals_sorted[i][1]
           suffix_min[i] = r if r < suffix_min[i+1] else suffix_min[i+1]
       # We need evaluate x at left endpoints <= Rmin and >= T? Actually x can be any integer in [T,Rmin]. For each p = number of intervals with l <= x, Rright = suffix_min[p]. The best x for that p is the smallest x with that p, which is l_sorted[p-1] (if p>0) or maybe T if T > l_p? But we argued if suffix_min[p]>=Lmax then l_p>=T. However for computing max f, we can simply iterate over possible x = l_sorted[i] for i=0..m-1 with l<=Rmin and l>=T? Let's be precise with duplicates and p.
       # Simpler: We can evaluate at every left endpoint l_i (unique maybe) <= Rmin. Also maybe x=T if T is not a left endpoint? T is max l of bad intervals, so it is a left endpoint. But if T<1? T>=1 because l>=1. So T is a left endpoint. Good.
       # For each unique l_val <= Rmin, let p = number of intervals with l <= l_val (including duplicates). Rright = suffix_min[p] (min r among intervals with l > l_val). If Rright >= Lmax, candidate = Rright - l_val.
       # But what about x values in (l_val, next_l) with same p? f smaller, so ignore.
       # What about x = T if T is not a left endpoint? T is max l of bad intervals, yes a left endpoint. If T < first l? no.
       # What about x=1 if first l>1 and p=0? In hard case Rmin<Lmax, p=0 infeasible because Rright=Rmin<Lmax. But if T maybe? T>=first l? Since bad interval with r<Lmax exists; its l could be > first l? T is max l of bad, not necessarily first. p=0 (x<first l) has Rright=Rmin<Lmax, infeasible. So ignore.
       # Need consider x values less than first l but >=T? T cannot be less than first l if first l is bad? If first l not bad and T maybe from later bad, T>first l, so x<T for p=0. infeasible. Good.
       best = -INF
       # Iterate through sorted intervals, grouping by l maybe.
       i=0
       while i<m:
           l_val = intervals_sorted[i][0]
           # skip duplicates? We need p = count of intervals with l <= l_val, which after grouping is i + count.
           j=i
           while j<m and intervals_sorted[j][0]==l_val:
               j+=1
           p = j  # number with l <= l_val
           if l_val <= Rmin:
               if suffix_min[p] >= Lmax:
                   cand = suffix_min[p] - l_val
                   if cand > best: best = cand
           i=j
       # Also consider x = Rmin if Rmin is not a left endpoint? For a given p, best x is left boundary l_p. If Rmin lies inside a step, the left boundary is <=Rmin and already considered. If the step starts at T which is a left endpoint. So no.
       # But what if the feasible x interval [T,Rmin] starts at T which is not the left boundary of the p-step? T is a left endpoint of a bad interval, but if there are intervals with same l? It is a left endpoint. The p-step left boundary is the l value where p count reached. If T lies inside a step (i.e., T is not the smallest x for that p), then the smallest x for that p is some l_p < T, but suffix_min[p] < Lmax at l_p? Wait if suffix_min[p]>=Lmax, then l_p>=T as argued. If suffix_min[p]<Lmax, no feasible. If T lies inside a step with suffix_min[p]>=Lmax, then l_p>=T contradiction. So T is exactly a step boundary for feasible steps. Good.
       return best > D

Pseudo:

class Solution:
    def minDifference(self, nums: List[int]) -> int:
        n = len(nums)
        known = [(i,v) for i,v in enumerate(nums) if v != -1]
        if not known:
            return 0

        def feasible(D):
            # check known-known adjacent no missing? We'll scan known positions.
            # Build intervals_C, intervals_F
            intervals_C = []
            intervals_F = []
            far_possible = True

            # prefix
            first_idx, first_val = known[0]
            if first_idx > 0:
                intervals_C.append(interval(first_val, D))
                intervals_F.append(interval(first_val, D))
            # iterate consecutive knowns
            for a in range(len(known)-1):
                idx1, val1 = known[a]
                idx2, val2 = known[a+1]
                gap = idx2 - idx1 - 1
                if gap == 0:
                    if abs(val1 - val2) > D:
                        return False
                elif gap == 1:
                    J = intersect_intervals(val1, val2, D)
                    if J is None:
                        return False
                    intervals_C.append(J)
                    intervals_F.append(J)
                else: # gap >=2
                    I1 = interval(val1, D)
                    I2 = interval(val2, D)
                    intervals_C.append(I1)
                    intervals_C.append(I2)
                    J = intersect_intervals(val1, val2, D)
                    if J is None:
                        far_possible = False
                    else:
                        intervals_F.append(J)
            # suffix
            last_idx, last_val = known[-1]
            if last_idx < n - 1:
                intervals_C.append(interval(last_val, D))
                intervals_F.append(interval(last_val, D))

            # Mode C
            if two_point(intervals_C, D, require_far=False):
                return True
            # Mode F
            if far_possible and two_point(intervals_F, D, require_far=True):
                return True
            return False

Need define interval(val,D): [max(1, val-D), val+D]. Since val-D may be <=0. But if val-D > val+D? D>=0, val-D <= val+D. If val-D<1, lower=1; upper val+D >= val >=1, nonempty.
intersect_intervals(val1,val2,D): I1=[max(1,val1-D), val1+D], I2=[max(1,val2-D), val2+D]. Intersection lower=max(l1,l2), upper=min(r1,r2). If lower<=upper return [lower,upper] else None.
But note if val1-D<1 and val2-D<1, lower=1. Good.

Potential issue: For a single gap with J nonempty, adding J to intervals_C and intervals_F. In Mode C (close), if |x-y|<=D, single gap condition is x∈J or y∈J. Yes. In Mode F, same. Good.

Potential issue: For a multi gap with J nonempty, in Mode C we add I1,I2 but not J. Suppose x,y close and neither x nor y in J, but x∈I1, y∈I2. Then intervals_C covered. Good. Suppose x,y close and x∈J, intervals_C covered. Good. Suppose x,y close and x∈I1, y∈I2 but |x-y|<=D; path works. Good.
In Mode F, we add J. Suppose x,y far and x∈J, y arbitrary. intervals_F covered. Good.

Now, two_point function details.

def two_point(intervals, D, require_far):
    if not intervals:
        return True
    Rmin = min(r for l,r in intervals)
    Lmax = max(l for l,r in intervals)
    if Rmin >= Lmax:
        return True
    # Rmin < Lmax
    T = -10**30
    for l,r in intervals:
        if r < Lmax:
            if l > T: T = l
    if T > Rmin:
        return False
    if not require_far:
        lower = max(1, T, Lmax - D)
        return lower <= Rmin
    else:
        # sort by l
        intervals.sort(key=lambda x: x[0])  # can mutate? intervals local, okay.
        m = len(intervals)
        INF = 10**30
        suffix = [INF]*(m+1)
        for i in range(m-1, -1, -1):
            r = intervals[i][1]
            suffix[i] = r if r < suffix[i+1] else suffix[i+1]
        best = -INF
        i=0
        while i<m:
            l_val = intervals[i][0]
            j=i
            while j<m and intervals[j][0] == l_val:
                j += 1
            p = j
            if l_val <= Rmin:
                rr = suffix[p]
                if rr >= Lmax:
                    cand = rr - l_val
                    if cand > best:
                        best = cand
            i = j
        return best > D

Need consider if require_far and D maybe 0; best >0. If intervals hard, best positive? Since Rmin<Lmax and x<=Rmin, Rright>=Lmax, so cand >= Lmax - x >0. Good.

But wait: In far mode, if common intersection nonempty, we return True. But if common intersection nonempty and D is huge, y=x+D+1 might be >? no upper bound. Good.

Now, is there a case where Mode C close feasible with x,y close but x>y? We assume x<=y. Since intervals symmetric, if pair has x>y, swap. Our two_point close condition uses x<=y and y>=Lmax. If the smaller point is not the one covering left intervals? It still works; any feasible pair can be ordered. Good.

Now, let's think about possible bug in two_point far evaluation with duplicates and suffix_min.

Suppose intervals sorted by l, with duplicates. For l_val, p = number of intervals with l <= l_val (j after grouping). suffix_min[p] = min r among intervals with index >= p, i.e., l > l_val (since all duplicates included before p). Good. If there are intervals with same l but some have r < Lmax (bad), they are included in p (l<=x) for x=l_val, so not in Rright. Is that correct? At x=l_val, an interval with l_i = l_val contains x if x<=r_i. Since x=l_val <= r_i (because l<=r), yes it is covered by x. So it should not be in the right set. Thus p includes all duplicates. Good.

Now, what about x can be chosen as a value between l_val and next l, not equal to l_val. Then intervals with l_i = l_val are still covered by x (since l_i <= x) if x<=r_i. x<=Rmin<=r_i, yes. So p same. f smaller. So evaluating at l_val is best.

What about x can be chosen less than l_val but same p? No, p would be smaller. The step's left boundary is l_val. Good.

Now, if T > l_val for a step with suffix_min[p]>=Lmax? We argued impossible, but let's verify with duplicates/bad intervals. Suppose intervals:

If there are multiple bad intervals, T=max l bad. For any p with suffix_min[p]>=Lmax, all bad intervals have index < p (because if a bad interval had index >=p, its r<Lmax would make suffix_min[p]<Lmax). Thus their l <= l_p, so T<=l_p. Good.

Now, let's consider if there are known values with -1 between but gap length huge. Our algorithm doesn't depend on gap length except k=1 vs k>=2. Is that correct? For a gap with k>=2, the exact length doesn't matter; as long as at least two unknowns, we can transition between x and y. If k=2, one edge; if k>2, more edges. No additional constraints. What if k=2 and x/y close, but first and last unknown are adjacent; okay. What if k=2 and both endpoints covered by same value x, choose both x; okay. What if k=2 and x covers I_L, y covers I_R, but |x-y|<=D; okay. If k>2, same. So length beyond 1 irrelevant. This is a key insight.

Now, let's think about if a gap with k>=2 and |x-y|<=D but x/y cover I_L/I_R, and k=2, the sequence is first unknown value a, second b. If a=b, edge diff0; if a≠b, diff |x-y|<=D. Good. If k=3, sequence a, a, b or a,b,b; edges okay. So yes.

Now, let's consider if a gap with k>=2 and |x-y|>D but J contains x, choose all x. If k=2, sequence x,x; known edges okay. If J contains y, choose all y. If both, choose either. Good.

Now, let's think about if there are multiple missing blocks and x,y chosen; in Mode F, each multi gap may choose either x or y, but if a known value is between two gaps, one gap may use x adjacent to it, the other use y adjacent to it; both diffs <=D because x/y in respective J intervals. For a multi gap, if choose x, x∈J=I_L∩I_R, so x within D of both endpoints. Thus for a known endpoint shared by two gaps, if left gap uses x and right gap uses y, both x and y are within D of that known (because x∈J_left includes right endpoint? Wait J_left for gap between A and B includes I_A∩I_B; if B is shared and left gap uses x, x∈I_B. Right gap uses y, y∈I_B if y∈J_right includes I_B. So both within D of B. Good. No global assignment issue.

Now, let's think about if a single gap J interval and a multi gap J interval can be covered by different values; two-point stabbing handles.

Now, let's consider if Mode C intervals for multi gaps include I_L and I_R, but if x,y close and a known endpoint is shared by two multi gaps, one gap may use x adjacent to endpoint, another use y adjacent to endpoint; both x,y within D of endpoint because each gap's endpoint interval I_endpoint is in intervals_C and covered by at least one of x,y. But does intervals_C ensure both x and y individually within D of endpoint if needed? It only ensures for each interval I_endpoint, at least one of x,y is in it. Suppose two gaps share endpoint B. Gap1 uses x adjacent to B, so need x∈I_B. Gap2 uses y adjacent to B, need y∈I_B. If intervals_C has I_B twice (once for each gap), two-point stabbing with duplicate intervals only requires each duplicate interval contains x or y. It could be that both duplicates are covered by x, and y not in I_B. Then if gap2 wants to use y adjacent to B, it would fail. But can we choose assignments to avoid using y adjacent to B? Let's examine.

This is important! Our interval stabbing formulation for Mode C says for each multi gap, condition is (x∈I_L or y∈I_L) and (x∈I_R or y∈I_R). This condition is per gap, not per endpoint globally. If the same known endpoint B appears in two gaps, the conditions are:
Gap1 (A,B): (x∈I_A or y∈I_A) and (x∈I_B or y∈I_B).
Gap2 (B,C): (x∈I_B or y∈I_B) and (x∈I_C or y∈I_C).
Thus I_B appears twice. Two-point stabbing with duplicate intervals requires for each occurrence, x∈I_B or y∈I_B. Since occurrences identical, this is equivalent to x∈I_B or y∈I_B. It does NOT require x∈I_B AND y∈I_B. But is it possible that satisfying both gaps requires both x and y in I_B? Let's see.

For Gap1, if x∈I_B, we can choose the unknown adjacent to B in Gap1 to be x (or y if y∈I_B). For Gap2, if x∈I_B, we can choose the unknown adjacent to B in Gap2 to be x. We can use x for both sides of B. There is no requirement to use y adjacent to B. The two unknowns are on opposite sides of B and not adjacent to each other. So if x∈I_B, both gaps can use x for the unknown next to B. Thus only need at least one value in I_B. Good. If x∈I_B but y not, choose x for both adjacent unknowns. No conflict. So duplicate intervals don't require both values. Good.

Similarly, for a single gap, the same unknown is adjacent to both endpoints, so need same value in J; interval J once. If two single gaps share endpoint B, each has its own unknown adjacent to B; they can use different values or same. Conditions: Gap1 (A,B) requires x∈J1 or y∈J1; Gap2 (B,C) requires x∈J2 or y∈J2. If x∈J1 and y∈J2, then for Gap1 unknown adjacent to B uses x (x∈I_B because J1 subset I_B); for Gap2 unknown adjacent to B uses y (y∈I_B because J2 subset I_B). Both x,y within D of B. But interval I_B is not explicitly in the list for single gaps; J1 and J2 each imply their covering value is in I_B. If x covers J1, x∈I_B; if y covers J2, y∈I_B. So both values may be in I_B. But our interval list has J1,J2; two-point stabbing might choose x∈J1, y∈J2. Then indeed both x,y in I_B. If it chooses x∈J1 and x∈J2 (if common), then only x in I_B. That's okay; both gaps use x adjacent to B. So no issue.

Now, consider a multi gap and a single gap sharing endpoint B. Multi gap condition includes I_B; single gap condition includes J_single subset I_B. If x covers I_B for multi gap and y covers J_single, then y∈I_B too. If x covers both, okay. No conflict.

Thus interval stabbing per gap is sufficient.

Let's write efficient code:

known_idx = []
known_val = []
for i,v in enumerate(nums):
    if v != -1:
        known_idx.append(i); known_val.append(v)
if not known_idx: return 0
k = len(known_idx)

def add_interval(arr, val, D):
    l = val - D
    if l < 1: l = 1
    r = val + D
    arr.append((l,r))  # tuple

def intersect_interval(val1,val2,D):
    l1 = val1 - D
    if l1 < 1: l1 = 1
    r1 = val1 + D
    l2 = val2 - D
    if l2 < 1: l2 = 1
    r2 = val2 + D
    l = l1 if l1 > l2 else l2
    r = r1 if r1 < r2 else r2
    if l <= r:
        return (l,r)
    return None

In feasible:
intervals_C = []
intervals_F = []
far_possible = True

if known_idx[0] > 0:
   add_interval(intervals_C, known_val[0], D)
   add_interval(intervals_F, known_val[0], D)

for a in range(k-1):
   gap = known_idx[a+1] - known_idx[a] - 1
   v1=known_val[a]; v2=known_val[a+1]
   if gap == 0:
       if abs(v1-v2) > D: return False
   elif gap == 1:
       J = intersect_interval(v1,v2,D)
       if J is None: return False
       intervals_C.append(J); intervals_F.append(J)
   else:
       add_interval(intervals_C, v1, D)
       add_interval(intervals_C, v2, D)
       J = intersect_interval(v1,v2,D)
       if J is None:
           far_possible = False
       else:
           intervals_F.append(J)

if known_idx[-1] < n-1:
   add_interval(intervals_C, known_val[-1], D)
   add_interval(intervals_F, known_val[-1], D)

if two_point(intervals_C, D, False): return True
if far_possible and two_point(intervals_F, D, True): return True
return False

Let's design feasible efficiently:

def feasible(D):
    intervals_C = []
    intervals_F = []
    far_possible = True

    # prefix
    if known_idx[0] > 0:
        v = known_val[0]
        l = v - D
        if l < 1: l = 1
        r = v + D
        intervals_C.append((l,r))
        intervals_F.append((l,r))

    for i in range(k-1):
        gap = known_idx[i+1] - known_idx[i] - 1
        v1 = known_val[i]; v2 = known_val[i+1]
        if gap == 0:
            if v1 - v2 > D or v2 - v1 > D:
                return False
        elif gap == 1:
            l1 = v1 - D
            if l1 < 1: l1 = 1
            r1 = v1 + D
            l2 = v2 - D
            if l2 < 1: l2 = 1
            r2 = v2 + D
            l = l1 if l1 > l2 else l2
            r = r1 if r1 < r2 else r2
            if l > r:
                return False
            intervals_C.append((l,r))
            intervals_F.append((l,r))
        else:
            # add I1, I2 to C
            l1 = v1 - D
            if l1 < 1: l1 = 1
            r1 = v1 + D
            intervals_C.append((l1,r1))
            l2 = v2 - D
            if l2 < 1: l2 = 1
            r2 = v2 + D
            intervals_C.append((l2,r2))
            # J
            l = l1 if l1 > l2 else l2
            r = r1 if r1 < r2 else r2
            if l <= r:
                intervals_F.append((l,r))
            else:
                far_possible = False

    if known_idx[-1] < n - 1:
        v = known_val[-1]
        l = v - D
        if l < 1: l = 1
        r = v + D
        intervals_C.append((l,r))
        intervals_F.append((l,r))

    if two_point(intervals_C, D, False):
        return True
    if far_possible and two_point(intervals_F, D, True):
        return True
    return False

Note: For gap>=2, J lower = max(l1,l2), upper=min(r1,r2). Since l1/l2 clipped to1. Good.

Now, two_point function:

def two_point(intervals, D, require_far):
    if not intervals:
        return True
    Rmin = 10**30
    Lmax = 0
    for l,r in intervals:
        if r < Rmin: Rmin = r
        if l > Lmax: Lmax = l
    if Rmin >= Lmax:
        return True
    T = 0
    for l,r in intervals:
        if r < Lmax and l > T:
            T = l
    if T > Rmin:
        return False
    if not require_far:
        lower = Lmax - D
        if T > lower: lower = T
        if lower < 1: lower = 1
        return lower <= Rmin
    # far
    intervals.sort()
    m = len(intervals)
    suffix = [0]*(m+1)
    INF = 10**30
    suffix[m] = INF
    for i in range(m-1, -1, -1):
        r = intervals[i][1]
        suffix[i] = r if r < suffix[i+1] else suffix[i+1]
    best = -INF
    i = 0
    while i < m:
        l_val = intervals[i][0]
        j = i + 1
        while j < m and intervals[j][0] == l_val:
            j += 1
        if l_val <= Rmin:
            rr = suffix[j]
            if rr >= Lmax:
                cand = rr - l_val
                if cand > best:
                    best = cand
        i = j
    return best > D

Need ensure T initialized to 0. Intervals l>=1, so T=0 means no bad? But in hard Rmin<Lmax, there is at least one bad, so T set >0. If no bad, T=0 and Rmin<Lmax impossible. If T=0 and require_far, sorting etc. But safe. If T=0 and lower maybe max(1,0,Lmax-D). Good.

Now, let's think about if intervals can have l=0? We clip to1, so no. T=0 sentinel okay.

Now, let's think about if suffix array allocation per far check O(m) memory. Fine. Could avoid suffix array by precomputing suffix min in list. Need suffix[j] for arbitrary j after grouping. We can compute suffix list. Good.

Could compute best without suffix array by scanning from right and maintaining? Since we need suffix min after each group. We can sort and compute suffix min list; okay.

Alternative: We can compute suffix_min in a list of length m+1; for m up to 1e5, fine.

Now, let's think about if in far mode, best might remain -INF if no l_val<=Rmin with rr>=Lmax. But if T<=Rmin, there should be at least one feasible x? Wait T<=Rmin ensures there exists x=T with Rright(T)>=Lmax. T is a left endpoint of a bad interval, and T<=Rmin. At x=T, p includes intervals with l<=T; suffix_min[p]>=Lmax. So our evaluation at l_val=T will find rr>=Lmax. But what if T is not a unique left endpoint? It is; we evaluate. So best set. Good.

If shared endpoint B, x∈I_B from first gap, y∈I_B from second gap if y covers I_B in second gap. But intervals_C only ensures each I_B interval covered by at least one of x/y. It could be that both I_B intervals are covered by x, and y not in I_B. Then for second gap, if y covers I_C but not I_B, we can choose the unknown adjacent to B in second gap to be x (since x∈I_B), and the unknown adjacent to C to be y (if y∈I_C). For a multi gap with k>=2, the first unknown adjacent to B can be x, last unknown adjacent to C can be y, and edge x-y <=D. Thus we don't need y∈I_B. So per gap endpoint coverage by at least one value is sufficient; the same value x can be used for all endpoints it covers. There is no global assignment conflict because each gap's endpoints can choose independently from the set of values covering them. If x covers I_B in both gaps, use x adjacent to B in both. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is a possible issue with the code if
</think>

=== ANSWER ===
```python
from typing import List

class Solution:
    def minDifference(self, nums: List[int]) -> int:
        n = len(nums)
        known_idx = []
        known_val = []
        for i, v in enumerate(nums):
            if v != -1:
                known_idx.append(i)
                known_val.append(v)

        if not known_idx:
            return 0

        def two_points(intervals, D, require_far):
            if not intervals:
                return True

            Rmin = 10**30
            Lmax = 0
            for l, r in intervals:
                if r < Rmin:
                    Rmin = r
                if l > Lmax:
                    Lmax = l

            # One value can cover all intervals.
            if Rmin >= Lmax:
                return True

            # T = max left endpoint among intervals whose right endpoint is < Lmax.
            T = 0
            for l, r in intervals:
                if r < Lmax and l > T:
                    T = l

            if T > Rmin:
                return False

            if not require_far:
                # Need x <= Rmin, x >= T, and Lmax - x <= D.
                lower = Lmax - D
                if T > lower:
                    lower = T
                if lower < 1:
                    lower = 1
                return lower <= Rmin

            # Far mode: need two points with distance > D.
            intervals.sort()
            m = len(intervals)
            INF = 10**30
            suffix_min = [INF] * (m + 1)

            for i in range(m - 1, -1, -1):
                r = intervals[i][1]
                suffix_min[i] = r if r < suffix_min[i + 1] else suffix_min[i + 1]

            best = -INF
            i = 0
            while i < m:
                l_val = intervals[i][0]
                j = i + 1
                while j < m and intervals[j][0] == l_val:
                    j += 1

                if l_val <= Rmin:
                    rr = suffix_min[j]
                    if rr >= Lmax:
                        cand = rr - l_val
                        if cand > best:
                            best = cand

                i = j

            return best > D

        def feasible(D):
            intervals_close = []
            intervals_far = []
            far_possible = True

            # Prefix before the first known value.
            if known_idx[0] > 0:
                v = known_val[0]
                l = v - D
                if l < 1:
                    l = 1
                r = v + D
                intervals_close.append((l, r))
                intervals_far.append((l, r))

            k = len(known_idx)

            for i in range(k - 1):
                gap = known_idx[i + 1] - known_idx[i] - 1
                v1 = known_val[i]
                v2 = known_val[i + 1]

                if gap == 0:
                    if abs(v1 - v2) > D:
                        return False

                elif gap == 1:
                    l1 = v1 - D
                    if l1 < 1:
                        l1 = 1
                    r1 = v1 + D

                    l2 = v2 - D
                    if l2 < 1:
                        l2 = 1
                    r2 = v2 + D

                    l = l1 if l1 > l2 else l2
                    r = r1 if r1 < r2 else r2

                    if l > r:
                        return False

                    intervals_close.append((l, r))
                    intervals_far.append((l, r))

                else:
                    # gap >= 2
                    l1 = v1 - D
                    if l1 < 1:
                        l1 = 1
                    r1 = v1 + D

                    l2 = v2 - D
                    if l2 < 1:
                        l2 = 1
                    r2 = v2 + D

                    intervals_close.append((l1, r1))
                    intervals_close.append((l2, r2))

                    l = l1 if l1 > l2 else l2
                    r = r1 if r1 < r2 else r2

                    if l <= r:
                        intervals_far.append((l, r))
                    else:
                        far_possible = False

            # Suffix after the last known value.
            if known_idx[-1] < n - 1:
                v = known_val[-1]
                l = v - D
                if l < 1:
                    l = 1
                r = v + D
                intervals_close.append((l, r))
                intervals_far.append((l, r))

            # Case 1: |x - y| <= D
            if two_points(intervals_close, D, False):
                return True

            # Case 2: |x - y| > D
            if far_possible and two_points(intervals_far, D, True):
                return True

            return False

        lo, hi = 0, 10**9
        while lo < hi:
            mid = (lo + hi) // 2
            if feasible(mid):
                hi = mid
            else:
                lo = mid + 1

        return lo
```
